And so thy sophistry’s o’erthrown.
But yet we are beside the question

Which thou didst raise the first contest on;

For that was, Whether bears are better

Than synod-men? I say, *Negatur*.

That bears are beasts, and synods men,

Is held by all: they’re better then;

For bears and dogs on four legs go,

As beasts, but synod-men on two.

’Tis true, they all have teeth and nails;

But prove that synod-men have tails;

Or that a rugged, shaggy fur

Grows o’er the hide of presbyter;

Or that his snout and spacious ears

Do hold proportion with a bear’s.

A bear’s a savage beast, of all

Most ugly and unnatural

Whelp’d without form, until the dam

Has lick’d it into shape and frame:

But all thy light can ne’er evict,

That ever synod man was lick’d,

Or brought to any other fashion

Than his own will and inclination.
But thou dost further yet in this

Oppugn thyself and sense; that is,

Thou would’st have presbyters to go

For bears and dogs, and bear-wards too;

A strange chimera of beasts and men,

Made up of pieces heterogene;

Such as in nature never met

In *eodem subjecto* yet.

Thy other arguments are all

Supposures, hypothetical,

That do but beg, and we may chose

Either to grant them, or refuse.

===Chinese===

统统虚假，根基全在误会：

我要带你连同你那一包袱

诡辩，一道打回谬误录；

再把你的论证装进

格式的框子，叫人看得明白。

我要用正当的推理逼你

撇下你那套诉词缠讼，81

叫你紧扣辩题，

按辩证法开讲。

那么先把辩题立起来：

熊与宗教会议，孰优

孰劣？熊劣，

我认；会议劣，你认。

而你要坐实这断言，

便说二者本是一回事。

若真是一回事，便无所谓劣；

因为既是 idem（同一），

*tantundem dat tantidem*，等量必生等量。

既然二者相同，

自然无分高下。

可我否认二者相同，

这话比否认蛆与我相同还难成立。

二者皆属 animalia（动物），

我认；rationalia（理性动物）则不敢恭维：

因为虽然同属一纲，

种差却历历可考；

断不能把会议说成熊，

正如不能证明我的马是苏格拉底。

至于宗教会议也是斗熊场，

你一口咬定；我说不然：

一个字便可证明：

凡集会而无权

惩戒、咒诅、赦罪、按立的，

就算不得宗教会议：而斗熊场

并无这些权柄；所以它不是：

你的诡辩就此倾覆。

不过我们仍旧跑题了，

你最初挑起争端的题面

是：熊是否优于

与会诸公？我说 Negatur，否。

熊是兽，与会者是人，

这是众所公认的；那么人更优；

因为熊和狗四条腿走路，

是兽；与会诸公却是两条腿。

不错，二者都有牙有爪；

但你且证明与会诸公有尾巴；

证明长老皮上

长着又粗又乱的毛；

证明他的口鼻和招风耳

同熊的比例相称。

熊是猛兽，一切兽中

最丑陋、最悖逆天性的，

产下时没有形状，须得

熊母亲把它舔出个模样：

而你的灵光再亮，也证明不了

哪位与会者曾被舔过，

或被舔成过他自己

意愿之外的任何形状。

更有甚者，你在这上头

自打耳光、违背常识；这就是说，

你要长老们

既算熊算狗，又算熊倌；

一具人兽合体的怪物，

由异质部件拼成；

这样的东西，自然界

从未在同一个体上凑齐过。

===Original===

Much thou hast said, which I know when

And where thou stol’st from other men,

Whereby ’tis plain thy light and gifts

Are all but plagiary shifts;

And is the same that Ranter said,

Who, arguing with me, broke my head,

And tore a handful of my beard:

The self-same cavils then I heard,

When, b’ing in hot dispute about

This controversy, we fell out:

And what thou know’st I answer’d then,

Will serve to answer thee agen.
Quoth Ralpho, Nothing but th’ abuse

Of human learning you produce;

Learning, that cobweb of the brain,

Profane, erroneous, and vain;

A trade of knowledge, as replete

As others are with fraud and cheat;

An art t’ encumber gifts and wit,

And render both for nothing fit;

Makes light unactive, dull, and troubled,

Like little David in Saul’s doublet:

A cheat that scholars put upon

Other men’s reason and their own;

A fort of error, to ensconce

Absurdity and ignorance;

That renders all the avenues

To truth impervious and abstruse,

By making plain things, in debate,

By art, perplex’d, and intricate:

For nothing goes for sense or light

That will not with old rules jump right:

As if rules were not in the schools

Deriv’d from truth, but truth from rules.

This Pagan heathenish invention

Is good for nothing but contention.

For as, in sword and buckler fight,

All blows do on the target light;

So when men argue, the great’st part

O’ th’ contest falls on terms of art,

Until the fustian stuff be spent,

And then they fall to th’ argument.
Quoth Hudibras, Friend Ralph, thou hast

Out-run the constable at last:

For thou art fallen on a new

Dispute, as senseless as untrue,

But to the former opposite

And contrary as black to white;

Mere disparata;82 that concerning

Presbytery; this, human learning;

Two things s’ averse, they never yet

But in thy rambling fancy met.

But I shall take a fit occasion

T’ evince thee by ratiocination,

Some other time, in place more proper

Than this we’re in; therefore let’s stop here,

And rest our weary’d bones a while,

Already tir’d with other toil.

===Chinese===

你其余的论证全是

假定之词、假设之法，

不过乞题为论，我们尽可

随意认下或驳回。

你说的这一大套，我知道

你是何时何处从别人那里偷来的，

可见你的灵光和恩赐

全是不折不扣的剽窃；

同那位狂热派说的如出一辙，

那人跟我辩论输了，

打破了我的头，

还薅走一把胡子：

当时的那些歪缠我全听过，

为这场争辩我们

吵得不可开交：

而你知道我当时如何回敬，

拿来回敬你，绰绰有余。

拉尔福道：您搬弄的

无非是对人文学的诋毁；

学问，那脑髓里的蛛网，

渎神、谬误而空洞；

一门知识的行当，同

别的行当一样充斥欺瞒；

一门捆住恩赐和才智的手艺，

叫两样东西一齐报废；

叫灵光呆滞、滞重、浑浊，

像小大卫套上扫罗的战袍：

是读书人加在

别人的理性、也加在自家理性上的骗局；

一座谬误的堡垒，专供

荒唐与无知龟缩；

它把通往真理的

一切门径尽数堵死弄玄，

办法是把明明白白的事，一到辩论

就被手艺搅成乱麻：

因为凡不合老规则的，

一概不算理性和光：

仿佛学堂里的规则

不是从真理推来，倒是真理从规则推来。

这项异教邪门的发明，

除吵架外一无是处。

正如剑盾之争，

招招都落在盾上；

世人辩论，十之八九

也耗在行话上，

等这匹粗糙的衣料扯完，

才轮到正题登场。

休迪布拉斯道：拉尔福老友，

你这回可花过了手头的钱：

因为你滑进了另一场

新争端，同样荒谬失实，

却同前一个争端针锋相对，

黑白相反；

纯然 disparata（不相干）；82那一个论的是

长老会，这一个论的是人文学；

两件事如此相克，素来

只在你信马由缰的想象里相逢。

且待我择个良机，

用推理给你上一课，

换个比眼下这地方

更相宜的场合；就此打住吧，

让劳乏的骨头歇一歇，

它们早已被别的苦役拖乏了。

To take in dudgeon, is inwardly to resent some injury or affront; a sort of grumbling in the gizzard, and what is previous to actual fury. ↩︎

The Heathens had an odd opinion, and have a strange reason why Moses imposed the law of circumcision on the Jews, which, how untrue soever, I will give the learned reader an account of without translation, as I find it in the annotations upon Horace, wrote by my worthy and learned friend Mr. William Baxter, the great restorer of the ancient and promoter of modern learning.

Vocem —﻿—﻿— qua inscitia Librarii exciderat reposuimus ex conjectura, uti et medicinaliter exsectus pro medicinalis effectus quae nihil erant. Cerberus; a name which poets give a dog with three heads, which they feigned doorkeeper of hell, that caressed the unfortunate souls sent thither, and devoured them that would get out again; yet Hercules tied him up, and made him follow. This dog with three heads denotes the past, the present, and the time to come; which receive, and, as it were, devour all things. Hercules got the better of him, which shows that heroic actions are always victorious over time, because they are present in the memory of posterity. ↩︎

14. Sceptic. Pyrrho was the chief of the sceptic Philosophers, and was at first, as Apollodorus saith, a painter, then became the hearer of Driso, and at last the disciple of Anaxagoras, whom he followed into India, to see the Gymnosophists. He pretended that men did nothing but by custom; there was neither honesty nor dishonesty, justice nor injustice, good nor evil. He was very solitary, lived to be ninety years old, was highly esteemed in his country, and created chief priest. He lived in the time of Epicurus and Theophrastus, about the 120th Olympiad. His followers were called Phyrrhonians; besides which they were named the Ephecticks and Aphoreticks, but more generally sceptics. This sect made their chiefest good to consist in a sedateness of mind, exempt from all passions; in regulating their opinions, and moderating their passions, which they called Ataxia and Metriopathia; and in suspending their judgment in regard of good and evil, truth or falsehood, which they called Epechi. Sextus Empiricus, who lived in the second century, under the Emperor Antoninus Pius, writ ten books against the mathematicians or astrologers, and three of the Phyrrhonian opinion. The word is derived from the Greek σκέπτομαι, quod est, *considerare*, *speculare*. ↩︎

14. 怀疑论者（Sceptic）。皮浪是怀疑派哲学家的翘楚，据阿波罗多罗斯说，他起先是个画家，继而在德里索门下听讲，终成阿那克萨戈拉的弟子，并随他远赴印度拜谒"裸形智者"。他声称人事全凭习惯：无所谓诚实与不诚实、正义与不义、善与恶。他极为孤僻，享年九十，在本邦深受敬重，还被推为大祭司。他与伊壁鸠鲁、泰奥弗拉斯托斯同时，约当第120届奥林匹克赛会之期。其追随者称皮浪派；此外还有"悬断派"（Ephecticks）与"搁置派"（Aphoreticks）之名，而以怀疑派通称。这一派把至善置于心灵的静定，超脱一切激情；置于节制意见、节制情欲，他们称之为 Ataxia 与 Metriopathia；置于对善恶真假悬置判断，称之为 Epechi。塞克斯都·恩披里柯生活于二世纪安东尼努斯·庇护帝之世，著有十卷驳数学家（即占星家）之书、三卷论皮浪派学说之书。此词源自希腊文 σκέπτομαι，意即*细察*（considerare）、*观照*（speculare）。 ↩︎

The old philosophers thought to extract notions out of natural things, as chymists do spirits and essences; and, when they had refined them into the nicest subtleties, gave them as insignificant names as those operators do their extractions: But (as Seneca says) the subtler things are they are but the nearer to nothing. So are all their definitions of things by acts the nearer to nonsense. ↩︎

19. Thomas Aquinas, a Dominican friar, was born in 1224, and studied at Cologne and Paris. He new-modelled the school divinity, and was therefore called the Angelic Doctor, and Eagle of Divines. The most illustrious persons of his time were ambitious of his friendship, and put a high value on his merits, so that they offered him bishoprics, which he refused with as much ardour as others seek after them. He died in the fiftieth year of his age, and was canonized by Pope John XII. We have his works in eighteen volumes, several times printed.
 Johannes Dunscotas was a very learned man, who lived about the end of the thirteenth and beginning of the fourteenth century. The English and Scotch strive which of them shall have the honour of his birth. The English say, he was born in Northumberland: the Scots alledge he was born at Duns, in the Mers, the neighbouring county to Northumberland, and hence was called Dunscotas. Moreri, Buchanan, and other Scotch historians, are of this opinion, and for proof cite his epitaph:

Scotia me genuit, Anglia suscepit,

Gallia edocuit, Germania tenet.

Scotland bore me, England reared me,

旧时哲学家们想把"理念"从自然物里萃取出来，好比化学家蒸馏精气；待把理念提炼到至精至微，便给它们起上与那些蒸馏师给馏出物起的同样无意义的名字。可（如塞内加所言）事物越精微，便越接近于无。所以他们那套以"行为"界定事物的定义，也就越接近胡言。 ↩︎

19. 托马斯·阿奎那，多明我会修士，生于1224年，求学于科隆与巴黎。他重塑了经院神学，故称"天使博士"、"神学家之鹰"。当时最显赫的人物都以结交为荣，对其才德评价极高，以致愿以主教之位相许；而他辞谢的热切，不下于旁人钻营的热切。他五十岁上去世，被教皇约翰二十二世封圣。其著作十八卷，屡经刊印。
 约翰·邓斯·司各脱是一位极有学问的人，生活于十三世纪末、十四世纪初。英格兰人与苏格兰人争夺他出生地的荣誉。英格兰人说他生于诺森伯兰；苏格兰人则坚称他生于邓斯——与诺森伯兰毗邻的马尔斯郡的一个地方，"邓斯·司各脱"之名即由此而来。莫雷里、布坎南等苏格兰史家皆持此说，并引其墓志为证：

Scotia me genuit, Anglia suscepit,
Gallia edocuit, Germania tenet.

（苏格兰生我，英格兰养我，）

He died at Cologne, November 8, 1308. In the Supplement to Dr. Cave’s *Historia Literaria*, he is said to be extraordinary learned in physics, metaphysics, mathematics, and astronomy; that his fame was so great when at Oxford, that 30,000 scholars came thither to hear his lectures: that when at Paris, his arguments and authority carried it for the immaculate conception of the Blessed Virgin; so that they appointed a festival on that account, and would admit us scholars to degrees but such as were of this mind. He was a great opposer of Thomas Aquinas’s doctrine; and, for being a very acute logician, was called Doctor Subtilis; [Discriminating (or, literally, Slender) Teacher] which was the reason also, that an old punster always called him the Lathy Doctor. ↩︎

Sorbon was the first and most considerable college of the university of Paris, founded in time reign of St. Lewis, by Robert Sorbon, which name is sometimes given to the whole University of Paris, which was founded, about the year 741, by Charlemagne, at the persuasion of the learned Alcuinus, who was one of the first professors there; since which time it has been very famous. This college has been rebuilt with an extraordinary magnificence, at the charge of Cardinal Richlieu, and contains lodgings for thirty-six doctors, who are called the Society of Sorbon. Those which are received among them before they have received their doctor’s degree are only said to be of the Hospitality of Sorbon. Claud. Hemeraus de Acad. Paris. Spondan in Annal. ↩︎

他于1308年11月8日卒于科隆。卡夫博士《文学史》补编说他于物理学、形而上学、数学、天文学淹博非常；在牛津执教时名声之大，竟致三万学子负笈听讲；在巴黎时，凭其论证与威望，使"圣母无染原罪"之说获判成立，为此特设节庆，并规定不持此说的学者不得授学位。他是托马斯·阿奎那学说的死对头；又因逻辑精微无比，人称"精细博士"（Doctor Subtilis）——也因此之故，一位老牌的打趣专家总管他叫"瘦杆博士"。 ↩︎
20. 索邦是巴黎大学最早也最显赫的学院，建于圣路易王之世，创办人为罗贝尔·德·索邦；此名有时也用于指称整个巴黎大学。该大学约于741年由查理曼大帝听从博学的阿尔昆之劝而创立，阿尔昆即该校最早的教授之一；自此声誉不衰。此学院后由黎塞留红衣主教斥资重建，壮丽非凡，内设三十六位博士的居所，即所谓"索邦学社"；未得博士学位而先入社者，仅称"索邦客卿"。见克劳德·赫梅吕《巴黎学院考》、斯庞达《编年史》。 ↩︎

27. Taliacotius was an Italian surgeon, that found out a way to repair lost and decayed noses. This Taliacotius was chief surgeon to the Great Duke of Tuscany, and wrote a treatise, *De Curtis Membris*, [Of Cutoff Parts] which he dedicates to his great master wherein he not only declares the models of his wonderful operations in restoring of lost members, but gives you cuts of the very instruments and ligatures he made use of therein; from hence our Author (*cum poetica licentia*) has taken his simile. ↩︎
28. Aeneas was the son of Anchises and Venus; a Trojan, who, after long travels, came to Italy, and after the death of his father-in-law, Latinus, was made king of Latium, and reigned three years. His story is too long to insert here, and therefore I refer you to Virgil’s *Aeneids*. Troy being laid in ashes, he took his aged father Anchises upon his back, and rescued him from his enemies. But being too solicitous for his son and household gods, he lost his wife Creusa; which Mr. Dryden, in his excellent translation, thus expresseth:

Haste my dear father (’tis no time to wait,)

And load my shoulders with a willing freight.

Whate’er befalls, your life shall be my care;

One death, or one deliv’rance, we will share.

My hand shall lead our little son; and you,

My faithful consort, shall our steps pursue.

28. 埃涅阿斯是安喀塞斯与维纳斯之子，特洛伊人；历经长久漂泊抵达意大利，岳父拉提努斯死后受立为拉丁姆王，在位三年。他的故事太长，此处不能备载，请径参维吉尔《埃涅阿斯纪》。特洛伊化作灰烬时，他把年迈的父亲安喀塞斯驮上背，从敌手中救出；只因过于挂念儿子与家神，反把妻子克列乌莎丢了；此事德莱顿先生在其出色的译本中是这样写的：

"快，亲爱的父亲（此刻不是耽搁的时候），
把甘愿的行装压上我的肩头。
无论遭逢什么，您的性命由我照看；
同死同生，一同得救。
小儿子我亲手牵引；而你，
我忠实的妻，请随我们的脚步。"

49. Mamaluke is the name of the militia of the Sultans of Egypt. It signified a servant or soldier. They were commonly captives taken from amongst the Christians, and instructed in military discipline, and did not marry. Their power was great; for besides that the Sultans were chosen out of their body, they disposed of the most important offices of the kingdom. They were formidable about 200 years; ’till at last Selim, Sultan of the Turks, routed them, and killed their Sultan, near Aleppo, 1516, and so put an end to the empire of Mamalukes, which had lasted 267 years.

Prometheus was the son of Iapetus, and brother of Atlas, concerning whom the poets have feigned, that having first formed men of the earth and water, he stole fire from heaven to put life into them; and that having thereby displeased Jupiter, he commanded Vulcan to tie him to mount Caucasus with iron chains, and that a vulture should prey upon his liver continually: but the truth of the story is, that Prometheus was an astrologer, and constant in observing the stars upon that mountain; and, that, among other things, he found the art of making fire, either by the means of a flint, or by contracting the sunbeams in a glass. Bochart will have Magog, in the Scripture, to be the Prometheus of the Pagans.

普罗米修斯是伊阿佩托斯之子、阿特拉斯之弟。诗人们虚构他先以泥水造人，复自天上盗火赋人以生命；因此触怒朱庇特，朱庇特命伏尔甘以铁链锁其身于高加索山，并遣一鹫日夜啄食其肝。而故事的真相是：普罗米修斯乃星占学家，常年在山上观星；除其他发现外，他寻得取火之术——或击燧石，或以玻璃聚日光。博夏特则认定《圣经》中的玛各即异教徒的普罗米修斯。
 作者此处及前文，皆在讥刺那些"同情粉"与"武器膏"的崇拜者——那两样在当日声望极高，复得显赫的肯内尔姆·迪格比爵士力挺；他就此题著有专论，我相信他落笔时是当真的，虽然此道如今几乎已被逐出人间。 ↩︎

67. Gundibert is a feigned name, made use of by Sir William d’Avenant in his famous epic poem, so called; wherein you may find also that of his mistress. This poem was designed by the author to be an imitation of the English drama: it being divided into five books, as the other is into five acts; the cantos to be parallel of the scenes, with this difference, that this is delivered narratively, the other dialoguewise. It was ushered into the world by a large preface, written by Mr. Hobbes, and by the pens of two of our best poets, viz. Mr. Waller and Mr. Cowley, which one would have thought might have proved a sufficient defence and protection against snarling critics. Notwithstanding which, four eminent wits of that age (two of which were Sir John Denham and Mr. Donne) published several copies of verses to Sir William’s discredit, under this title, Certain Verses written by several of the Author’s Friends, to be reprinted with the second Edition of *Gundibert* in 8vo Lond. 1653. These verses were as wittily answered by the author, under this title, *The Incomparable Poem of Gundibert Vindicated from the Wit Combat of Four Esquires, Clinias, Damoetas, Sancho, and Jack-Pudding*; printed in 8vo Lond. 1665, Vide Langbain’s *Account of Dramatic Poets*. ↩︎

67. 贡迪伯特是威廉·达文南特爵士同名名作史诗中的虚构之名；其情娘之名亦见该书。作者原以此诗摹仿英国戏剧：全诗分五卷，如剧分五幕；歌拟场，所异者此以叙述出之，彼以对话出之。其问世有霍布斯先生长序开道，复有我们两位最佳诗人沃勒先生与考利先生执笔揄扬，照想足以抵御狂吠的批评家。虽如此，当年四位显赫才子（其二为约翰·德纳姆爵士与多恩先生）仍刊诗数首以损达文南特爵士，题曰《作者诸友所作诗若干》，随1653年伦敦八开本《贡迪伯特》二版重刊。作者以同样机智作答，题曰《无与伦比之诗〈贡迪伯特〉辩护，驳四骑士克利尼阿斯、达莫埃塔斯、桑丘与杰克布丁之才战》；1665年伦敦八开本。见朗贝恩《剧作家纪事》。 ↩︎

Sic Dii jubete, fiat ex Gallo Capus.

—﻿Thomas More.

79. Smectymnuus was a club of five parlimentary holders-forth; the characters of whose names and talents were by themselves expressed in that senseless and insignificant word. They wore handkerchiefs about their necks for a mark of distinction (as the Officers of the Parliament Army then did) which afterwards degenerated into carnal cravats. About the beginning of the long Parliament, in the year 1641, these five wrote a book against Episcopacy and the Common Prayer, to which they all subscribed their names; being Stephen Marshal, Edmund Calamy, Thomas Young, Matthew Newcomen, and William Spurstow, and from thence they and their followers were called Smectymnians. They are remarkable for another pious book, which they wrote some time after that, entitled, *The King’s Cabinet Unlocked*, wherein all the chaste and endearing expressions, in the letters that passed betwixt his Majesty King Charles I and his Royal Consort are by these painful labourers in the devil’s vineyard turned into burlesque and ridicule. Their books were answered with as much calmness and genteelness of expression, and as much learning and honesty, by the Rev. Mr. Symonds, then a deprived clergyman, as theirs was stuffed with malice, spleen, and rascally invectives. ↩︎

Platina saith she was of English extraction, but born at Mentz; who, having disguised herself like a man, travelled with her paramour to Athens, where she made such progress in learning, that coming to Rome, she met with few that could equal her; so that, on the death of Pope Leo IV she was chosen to succeed him; but being got with child by one of her domestics, her travail came upon her between the Colossian Theatre and St. Clement’s, as she was going to the Lateran Church, and died upon the place, having sat two years, one month, and four days, and was buried there without any pomp.

79. 斯梅克提姆努斯是五位议会派布道人的班子；他们姓氏与才具的字母，被他们自己拼成了那个索然无义的词。他们颈间系手帕为记（一如当年议会军的军官），后来这手帕堕落成了肉身的领带。约当长期议会之初的1641年，五人合著一书攻主教制与《公祷书》，联署其名：斯蒂芬·马歇尔、埃德蒙·卡拉米、托马斯·扬、马修·纽科门、威廉·斯珀斯托；自此他们及其党徒被称为斯梅克提姆努斯派。他们另有一部"敬虔"之作亦颇可观，题曰《国王的内阁开启》：查理一世与王后通信中那些贞洁缱绻之语，被这批魔鬼葡萄园里的苦力一一化为笑柄。其书由可敬的赛蒙兹先生——其时一位被褫夺圣职的教士——作答，其从容儒雅之辞、渊博诚笃之学，恰与他们满纸的恶毒、怨毒与无赖谩骂两相对照。 ↩︎
80. 此事关乎女教皇琼安，号约翰八世。普拉蒂纳言其为英格兰血统，生于美因茨；她男装与情夫同赴雅典，学问大进，至罗马几无敌手；故教皇利奥四世死后，被选为继任；后因与一仆珠胎暗结，于赴拉特兰教堂途中，在大竞技场与圣克莱门特教堂之间临产，当场殒命——在位二年一月又四日，就地草葬，了无仪卫。

Roger Bacon, commonly called Friar Bacon, lived in the reign of Edward I and, for some little skill he had in the mathematics, was by the rabble accounted a conjurer, and had the sottish story of the brazen head fathered upon him by the ignorant monks of those days. Robert Grosthead was bishop of Lincoln in the of Henry III. He was a learned man for those times, and for that reason suspected by the clergy to be a conjurer; for which crime, being degraded by Pope Innocent IV and summoned to appear at Rome, he appealed to the tribunal of Christ; which our lawyers say is illegal, if not a praemunire, for offering to sue in a Foreign Court. ↩︎

那以自然为根基、放之四海皆准的尺码；因为只要在绳端吊一重物摆动，依太阳或任一恒星之运行，算出摆动历时几何、与绳长摆重成何比例，便可倒推回去：由任意一段时长，算出任何一根恰好摆动这么久的绳子的确切长度；如此一来，倘有人在中国要买"一刻钟的缎子"或"一刻钟的塔夫绸"，店家也全然明白何意；全人类便学会一种新的度量法——不再论码、论尺、论寸，而是论时、论刻、论分。 ↩︎

219. Prester John, an absolute prince, emperor of Abyssinia or Ethiopia. One of them is reported to have had seventy kings for his vassals, and so superb and arrogant, that none durst look upon him without his permission. ↩︎
220. Joan of Arc, called also the Pucelle, or Maid of Orleans. She was born at the town of Damremi, on the Meuse, daughter of James de Arc, and Isabella Romée; and was bred up a shepherdess in the country. At the age of eighteen or twenty she pretended to an express commission from God to go to the relief of Orleans, then besieged by the English, and defended by John Compte de Dennis, and almost reduced to the last extremity. She went to the coronation of Charles the Seventh, when he was almost ruined. She knew that prince in the midst of his nobles; though meanly habited. The doctors of divinity, and members of parliament, openly declared that there was some thing supernatural in her conduct. She sent for a sword, which lay in the tomb of a knight, which was behind the great altar of the church of St. Katharine de Forbois, upon the blade of which the cross and flower-de-luces were engraven, which put the king in a very great surprise, in regard none besides himself knew of it. Upon this he sent her with the command of some troops, with which she relieved Orleans, and drove the English from it, defeated Talbot at the battle of Pattai, and recovered Champagne. At last she was unfortunately taken prisoner in a sally at Champagne in 1430, and tried for a witch or sorceress, condemned, and burnt in Rouen marketplace in May, 1430. ↩︎
221. The Salique law is a law in France, whereby it is enacted, that no female shall inherit that crown. ↩︎

220. 贞德，又称"少女"（la Pucelle），奥尔良之少女。生于默兹河畔栋雷米，雅克·德·阿克与伊莎贝拉·罗梅之女，自幼牧羊乡间。年十八九，自诩奉上帝明谕，往解被英军围困、由约翰·德尼伯爵死守、几近山穷水尽的奥尔良。她赴查理七世的加冕礼——其时君主已濒绝境。她能在满殿贵胄之中认出衣饰寒素的君王。神学博士与议会议员公开宣称，其行止有超自然者在。她遣人取来一剑，剑藏于一骑士墓中，在圣卡特琳·德福尔布瓦教堂大祭坛之后；剑刃镌有十字与百合花——国王为之大骇，盖此事除其本人外无人知晓。于是拨兵马付之；她解奥尔良之围，逐英军于城外，于帕提会战破塔尔博特，收复香槟。1430年终在香槟一次突围中不幸被俘，以女巫或妖术之罪受审定谳，1430年5月焚于鲁昂市集。 ↩︎
221. 萨利克法乃法兰西之法：女子不得继承王冠。 ↩︎

## The Author’s Life / 作者生平
===Original===

Samuel Butler, the Author of this excellent Poem, was born in the Parish of Strensham, in the county of Worcester, and baptized there the 13th of Feb. 1612. His father, who was of the same name, was an honest country farmer, who had some small estate of his own, but rented a much greater of the Lord of the Manor where he lived. However, perceiving in this son an early inclination to learning, he made a shift to have him educated in the free-school at Worcester, under Mr. Henry Bright; where having passed the usual time, and being become an excellent school-scholar, he went for some little time to Cambridge, but was never matriculated into that University, his father’s abilities not being sufficient to be at the charge of an academical education; so that our Author returned soon into his native county, and became clerk to one Mr. Jefferys, of Earl’s-Croom, an eminent Justice of the Peace for that County, with whom he lived some years, in an easy and no contemptible service. Here by the indulgence of a kind master, he had sufficient leisure to apply himself to whatever learning his inclinations led him, which were chiefly history and poetry; to which, for his diversion, he joined music and painting; and I have seen some pictures, said to be of his drawing, which remained in that family; which I mention not for the excellency of them, but to satisfy the reader of his early inclinations to that noble art; for which also he was afterwards entirely beloved by Mr. Samuel Cooper, one of the most eminent painters of his time.
He was after this recommended to that great encourager of learning, Elizabeth Countess of Kent, where he had not only the opportunity to consult all manner of learned books, but to converse also with that living library of learning, the great Mr. Selden.
Our Author lived some time also with Sir Samuel Luke, who was of an ancient family in Bedfordshire but, to his dishonour, an eminent commander under the usurper Oliver Cromwell: and then it was, as I am informed, he composed this loyal Poem. For, though fate, more than choice, seems to have placed him in the service of a Knight so notorious, both in his person and politics, yet, by the rule of contraries, one may observe throughout his whole Poem, that he was most orthodox, both in his religion and loyalty.

===Chinese===

塞缪尔·巴特勒（Samuel Butler），这部杰作的作者，生于伍斯特郡斯特伦舍姆教区，并于 1612 年 2 月 13 日在那里受洗。他的父亲与他同名，是个诚实的乡下庄稼人，自家有些薄产，但主要租种所居庄园领主的田地。不过，见这孩子早早显出向学的志趣，他还是设法送他进了伍斯特的免费学校，师从亨利·布莱特（Henry Bright）先生；在那里修完通常的年限、成为一名出色的学生之后，他又去剑桥逗留了短短一阵，但始终未在那所大学注册——父亲的家底供不起大学教育的开销。于是我们的作者不久便回到本郡，给厄尔斯克鲁姆的杰弗里斯先生（Mr. Jefferys）当文书；杰弗里斯是该郡有名的治安法官。作者在他门下过了几年，差事清闲而不失体面。在这里，承蒙主人宽厚，他有充裕的闲暇随心研习学问，主要是历史与诗歌；消遣之余，又兼习音乐与绘画。我见过几幅据说是他手笔的画，还留在那家人手里；提这一点并非说画得多好，只想让读者知道他早年对这门高贵艺术的志趣；也正因如此，他后来深得塞缪尔·库珀先生（Mr. Samuel Cooper）——他那个时代最负盛名的画家之一——的钟爱。

此后经人引荐，他投到学问的大赞助人肯特伯爵夫人伊丽莎白（Elizabeth, Countess of Kent）门下；在那里他不但有机会翻检各种学术书籍，还能与那座活的藏书楼、伟大的塞尔登先生（Mr. Selden）交谈。

我们的作者还在塞缪尔·卢克爵士（Sir Samuel Luke）府上待过一段时间。卢克家是贝德福德郡的旧族，只是很不光彩，他是篡位者奥利弗·克伦威尔麾下的一员显赫将领；而据我所知，作者正是在那段时间写成这首效忠王室的讽诗的。因为，尽管把他安插在这样一位无论人品还是政见都声名狼藉的骑士门下，与其说是他自己的选择，不如说是命运的摆布，然而按照“相反相成”的道理，通读全诗便可看出：他在宗教和忠诚两方面都是最正统的。

===Original===

And I am the more induced to believe he wrote it about that time, because he had then the opportunity to converse with those living characters of rebellion, nonsense, and hypocrisy, which he so livelily and pathetically exposes throughout the whole work.
After the restoration of King Charles II those who were at the helm, minding money more than merit, our Author found that verse in Juvenal to be exactly verified in himself:

> Haud facile emergunt, quorum virtutibus obstat

Res angusta domi:4

And being endued with that innate modesty, which rarely finds promotion in princes’ courts. He became Secretary to Richard Earl of Carbury, Lord President of the Principality of Wales, who made him Steward of Ludlow-Castle, when the Court there was revived. About this time he married one Mrs. Herbert, a gentlewoman of a very good family, but no widow, as the Oxford Antiquary has reported; she had a competent fortune, but it was most of it unfortunately lost, by being put out on ill securities, so that it was of little advantage to him. He is reported by the Antiquary to have been Secretary to his Grace George Duke of Buckingham, when he was Chancellor to the University of Cambridge; but whether that be true or no, it is certain, the Duke had a great kindness for him, and was often a benefactor to him. But no man was a more generous friend to him, than that Mecaenas of all learned and witty men, Charles Lord Buckhurst, the late Earl of Dorset and Middlesex, who, being himself an excellent poet, knew how to set a just value upon the ingenious performances of others, and has often taken care privately to relieve and supply the necessities of those, whose modesty would endeavour to conceal them; of which our author was a signal instance, as several others have been, who are now living. In fine the integrity of his life, the acuteness of his wit, and easiness of his conversation, had rendered him most acceptable to all men; yet he prudently avoided a multiplicity of acquaintance, and wisely chose such only whom his discerning judgment could distinguish (as Mr. Cowley expresseth it)

> From the great vulgar or the small.

===Chinese===

我之所以更倾向于相信他就是在那段时间写成此诗，是因为他当时正好得以同那些活生生的叛逆、荒唐与虚伪的角色朝夕相处，而全书中被他揭露得如此活灵活现、鞭辟入里的，正是这类人物。

查理二世复辟之后，当政诸公眼里只有钱、没有才，我们的作者发觉尤维纳利斯的诗句恰恰应在自己身上：

> 家境窘迫，纵有美德也难出头（Haud facile emergunt, quorum virtutibus obstat res angusta domi）：4

他天赋谦退，而这种品格在君王的宫廷里向来难得晋升。他做了威尔士亲王封国枢密院院长卡伯里伯爵理查（Richard, Earl of Carbury）的秘书；勒德洛城堡复设宫廷时，伯爵委任他为城堡总管。大约在这时，他娶了赫伯特夫人（Mrs. Herbert），一位出身极好人家的淑女——并非如牛津那位古物家所传是个寡妇；她带来一份可观的财产，可惜其中大半投在不可靠的担保上折损了，于他几乎无所助益。据那位古物家说，他还当过剑桥大学校长白金汉公爵乔治殿下的秘书；此说真伪姑且不论，公爵确乎对他青眼有加，时常周济他。但对他最慷慨的朋友，莫过于那位一切才智之士的梅塞纳斯——查理·萨克赫斯特勋爵（Charles, Lord Buckhurst），即已故的多塞特与米德尔塞克斯伯爵：他自己就是出色的诗人，深知如何公允掂量别人的精妙之作，而且常常暗中接济、贴补那些因自谦而羞于告贫之人的窘迫；我们的作者便是一个显著的例子，如今在世的此类人物还有好几位。总之，他立身的端正、才思的敏锐、谈吐的随和，使他人人乐于亲近；但他谨慎地不广交游，只择那些凭他慧眼足以甄别的人为友（用考利先生的话说），

> 既不属大俗人，也不属小俗人。

===Original===

And having thus lived to a good old age, admired by all, though personally known to few, he departed this life in the year 1680, and was buried at the charge of his good friend Mr. Longuevil, of the Temple, in the yard belonging to the church of St. Paul’s Covent-garden, at the west-end of the said yard, on the north side, under the wall of the said church, and under that wall which parts the yard from the common highway. And since he has no monument yet set up for him, give me leave to borrow his epitaph from that of Michael Drayton, the poet, as the author of Mr. Cowley’s has partly done before me:

> And though no monument can claim

To be the treasurer of thy name;

This work, which ne’er will die, shall be

An everlasting monument to thee.

===Chinese===

他就这样活到高寿，人人敬慕而少有人亲识，于 1680 年辞世，由他的好友、律师公会的朗厄维尔先生（Mr. Longuevil）出资安葬，葬在科文特花园圣保罗教堂附属墓地的西端、北侧，在该教堂的墙下、墓地与公共大道隔墙相接之处。既然至今还没有人给他立碑，请容我借用诗人迈克尔·德雷顿（Michael Drayton）的墓志铭来作他的墓志，正如考利先生墓志的作者在我之前已经部分做过的那样：

> 纵然没有碑石敢自称
>
> 是你声名的保管人；
>
> 这部永不会死的作品，将是你
>
> 一座永远的纪念碑。

### The Lady’s Answer to the Knight / 贵妇对骑士的回答
===Original===

That you’re a beast, and turn’d to grass,

Is no strange news, nor ever was;

At least to me, who once, you know,

Did from the pound replevin you,

When both your sword and spurs were won

In combat by an Amazon:

That sword, that did (like Fate) determine

Th’ inevitable death of vermin,

And never dealt its furious blows,

But cut the throats of pigs and cows,

By Trulla was, in single fight,

Disarm’d and wrested from its Knight;

Your heels degraded of your spurs,

And in the stocks close prisoners;

Where still they’d lain, in base restraint,

If I, in pity of your complaint,

Had not, on honourable conditions,

Releas’d ’em from the worst of prisons;

And what return that favour met

You cannot (though you would) forget;

When, being free, you strove t’ evade

The oaths you had in prison made;

Forswore yourself, and first deny’d it,

But after own’d and justify’d it;

And when y’ had falsely broke one vow,

Absolv’d yourself by breaking two:

For while you sneakingly submit,

And beg for pardon at our feet,

Discourag’d by your guilty fears,

To hope for quarter for your ears,

And doubting ’twas in vain to sue,

You claim us boldly as your due;

Declare that treachery and force,

To deal with us, is th’ only course;

We have no title nor pretence

To body, soul, or conscience;

But ought to fall to that man’s share

That claims us for his proper ware.

These are the motives which, t’ induce

Or fright us into love, you use;

A pretty new way of gallanting,

Between soliciting and ranting;

Like sturdy beggars, that entreat

For charity at once, and threat!

But since you undertake to prove

Your own propriety in love,

As if we were but lawful prize

In war between two enemies,

Or forfeitures, which ev’ry lover,

That would but sue for, might recover,

It is not hard to understand

The myst’ry of this bold demand,

That cannot at our persons aim,

But something capable of claim.
’Tis not those paltry counterfeit

French stones, which in our eyes you set,

But our right diamonds, that inspire

And set your am’rous hearts on fire:

Nor can those false St. Martin’s beads,

Which on our lips you lay for reds,

And make us wear, like Indian dames,

Add fuel to your scorching flames,

But those true rubies of the rock,

Which in our cabinets we lock.

’Tis not those orient pearls, our teeth,

That you are so transported with;

===Chinese===

说你本是畜生、如今改吃草了，

这可不是新闻，从来就不是；

至少对我而言——你知道，我当年

可是把你从牲畜栏里保释出来的，

那时你的剑和马刺

都在单打独斗里叫一位女将赢了去：

那柄剑，曾像命运之神一般

裁决害虫们不可避免的死，

而且从不出手则已，

一出手便是宰猪割牛的喉咙，

却被特鲁拉在单挑中

从它的骑士手里缴了械；

你的脚跟也被褫夺了马刺，

双双成了足枷里的囚徒；

若不是我怜悯你的哀告，

以体面的条件把他们

从最坏的牢狱里开释出来，

他们至今还窝在那儿受着窝囊罪呢；

而那份恩情换来了什么报答，

你想忘也忘不掉；

你一获自由，就拼命想赖掉

坐牢时立下的誓言；

赌了咒又自打嘴巴，先是不认账，

后来索性认下还要强辩；

你背弃了一誓，

就再背两誓来赦免自己：

因为你一面偷偷地服软，

跪在我们脚下讨饶，

一面被做贼心虚吓破了胆，

不指望耳朵能得饶恕，

又疑心苦苦哀求也是白搭，

便大胆把我们当作你应得之物来强要；

还宣称对付我们，

欺骗和强暴是唯一的正道；

说我们对身体、灵魂和良心

都没有半点权利和名分；

只该归到那个

把我们当作自家货物的男人名下。

这些就是你用来引诱

或吓唬我们堕入情网的手段；

好一种别致的新式求爱，

介于软求与咆哮之间；

像那些横壮的叫花子，一边乞讨

施舍，一边就威吓！

不过你既然要证明

你在爱情上的所有权，

仿佛我们只是两个敌国交兵时

合法的战利品，

或是没收品，哪个情人

只要肯告上一状就能领走，

那就不难看穿你这狂妄要求

里的名堂了：

它瞄的绝不是我们这个人，

而是某种可以主张权利的东西。

你在我们眼里安上的那些

廉价冒牌的法国水钻，

点不着你们的爱情之火；

点燃你们多情心肠的，

是我们货真价实的钻石：

你在我们唇上敷的那种

假圣马丁珠粉，叫我们像

印第安女人一样涂着，也休想

给你灼人的烈火添柴；

能添柴的是我们锁在

小柜里的真矿红宝石。

叫你神魂颠倒的

也不是那些东方明珠——我们的牙齿；

===Original===

But those we wear about our necks,

Produce those amorous effects.

Nor is ’t those threads of gold, our hair,

The periwigs you make us wear;

But those bright guineas in our chests,

That light the wild-fire in your breasts.

These love-tricks I’ve been vers’d in so,

That all their sly intrigues I know,

And can unriddle, by their tones,

Their mystic cabals and jargons;

Can tell what passions, by their sounds,

Pine for the beauties of my grounds;

What raptures fond and amorous

O’ th’ charms and graces of my house;

What ecstasy and scorching flame

Burns for my money in my name;

What from th’ unnatural desire

To beasts and cattle takes its fire;

What tender sigh, and trickling tear,

Longs for a thousand pounds a year;

And languishing transports are fond

Of statute, mortgage, bill, and bond.
These are th’ attracts which most men fall

Enamour’d, at first sight, withal;

To these th’ address with serenades,

And court with balls and masquerades;

And yet, for all the yearning pain

Y’ have suffer’d for their loves in vain,

I fear they’ll prove so nice and coy

To have, and t’ hold, and to enjoy,

That all your oaths and labour lost,

They’ll ne’er turn ladies of the post.

This is not meant to disapprove

Your judgment in your choice of love;

Which is so wise the greatest part

Of mankind study ’t as an art;

For love should, like a deodand,

Still fall to th’ owner of the land;

And where there’s substance for its ground,

Cannot but be more firm and sound

Than that which has the slightest basis

Of airy virtue, wit, and graces;

Which is of such thin subtlety,

It steals and creeps in at the eye,

And, as it can’t endure to stay,

Steals out again as nice a way.
But love, that its extraction owns

From solid gold and precious stones,

Must, like its shining parents, prove

As solid, and as glorious love.

Hence ’tis you have no way t’ express

Our charms and graces but by these:

For what are lips, and eyes, and teeth,

Which beauty invades and conquers with,

But rubies, pearls, and diamonds,

With which a philtre love commands?
This is the way all parents prove,

In managing their children’s love,

That force ’em t’ intermarry and wed,

As if th’ were burying of the dead;

Cast earth to earth, as in the grave,

To join in wedlock all they have,

And, when the settlement’s in force,

Take all the rest for better or worse:

===Chinese===

能生那般爱情奇效的，

是我们挂在脖子上的那些。

叫你们心头发烫的

也不是那些金丝——我们的头发，

和你们非叫我们戴的假发；

是我们钱箱里亮闪闪的基尼

点燃了你们胸中的野火。

这套爱情的把戏我见得多了，

那点鬼鬼祟祟的名堂我全懂，

凭他们的腔调就能破译

那些玄妙的密语和黑话：

听得出哪些情焰，隔着声声叹息，

害着相思，害的是我地产的丰姿；

哪些痴人说梦，为我宅邸的风韵

神魂颠倒；

哪股狂喜和灼人的烈火

烧的是署着我名字的钱财；

哪种不自然的欲火

是从牲口和走兽身上取的火种；

哪声温柔的叹息、哪滴滚落的泪，

渴望的是年入千镑；

哪些恹恹的痴醉

恋慕的是法定产业、抵押、票据和债券。

大多数男人一见倾心的

正是这些迷人处；

为这些他们唱小夜曲，

用舞会和化装舞会来追求；

然而，任凭你为他们空爱一场、

受尽刻骨的相思之苦，

我只怕论到占有、把持和享用，

他们偏又那般挑剔忸怩，

你的誓词和苦功全打了水漂，

她们到死也不肯做“驿站贵妇”。

这并不是要非议

你择爱的眼光；

这眼光如此明智，世人

大半把它当学问来研习；

因为爱情本该像“献神物”，

一律落进地产主人的腰包；

既有实打实的家底垫底，

这份爱情自然比那种

以轻飘飘的美德、才情和风韵

为薄薄根基的更牢靠；

那种货色细妙得没分量，

从眼睛里偷偷爬进去，

又因为它待不住，

照原路悄悄溜出来。

而出身于足金和宝石的爱情，

必然像它光辉的父母一样，

是足色的、辉煌的爱情。

所以你们除了拿这些宝贝，

再没法形容我们的魅力和风姿：

因为嘴唇、眼睛和牙齿——

美人攻城略地的兵器——

还不是红宝石、珍珠和钻石，

爱情拿它们当迷魂汤使？

天下父母管制儿女婚事，

用的正是这套办法：

逼他们嫁娶成婚，

像是在办理丧事；

土归了土，一如下葬，

把两家财产并作一家；

婚约一生效，

其余的一切也就好坏都照单全收：

===Original===

For money has a power above

The stars and fate to manage love,

Whose arrows, learned poets hold,

That never miss, are tipp’d with gold.218

And though some say the parents’ claims

To make love in their children’s names,

Who many times at once provide

The nurse, the husband, and the bride,

Feel darts and charms, attracts and flames,

And woo and contract in their names,

And as they christen, use to marry ’em,

And, like their gossips, answer for ’em;

Is not to give in matrimony,

But sell and prostitute for money;

’Tis better than their own betrothing,

Who often do ’t for worse than nothing;

And when th’ are at their own dispose,

With greater disadvantage choose.

All this is right; but for the course

You take to do ’t, by fraud or force,

’Tis so ridiculous, as soon

As told, ’tis never to be done,

No more than setters can betray,

That tell what tricks they are to play.

Marriage, at best, is but a vow,

Which all men either break or bow:

Then what will those forbear to do,

Who perjure when they do but woo?

Such as before-hand swear and lie

For earnest to their treachery,

And, rather than a crime confess,

With greater strive to make it less?

Like thieves, who, after sentence past,

Maintain their innocence to the last;

And when their crimes were made appear

As plain as witnesses can swear,

Yet, when the wretches come to die,

Will take upon their death a lie.

Nor are the virtues you confess’d

T’ your ghostly father, as you guess’d,

So slight as to be justify’d

By being as shamefully deny’d;

As if you thought your word would pass

Point-blank, on both sides of a case;

Or credit were not to be lost

B’ a brave Knight-Errant of the Post,

That eats perfidiously his word,

And swears his ears through a two-inch board;

Can own the same thing, and disown,

And perjure booty, pro and con;

Can make the Gospel serve his turn,

And help him out, to be forsworn;

When ’tis laid hands upon, and kist,

To be betray’d and sold, like Christ.

These are the virtues in whose name

A right to all the world you claim,

And boldly challenge a dominion,

In grace and nature, o’er all women;

Of whom no less will satisfy

Than all the sex your tyranny.

Although you’ll find it a hard province,

With all your crafty frauds and covins,

To govern such a num’rous crew,

Who, one by one, now govern you;

For if you all were Solomons,

===Chinese===

因为金钱驾驭爱情的本事，

比星宿和命运还要大；

博学的诗人们说过，百发百中的

丘比特之箭，箭头是黄金的。218

虽说有人讲，父母出面

替儿女谈情说爱，

常常一口气备齐了

奶妈、新郎和新娘，

自己来感受箭矢、媚惑、吸引和情焰，

用自己的名义求婚订约，

洗礼完了顺手就办婚礼，

还像教父教母一样替他们应承一切，

这并不是嫁女儿，

而是拿人换钱、拉皮条；

这总强过他们自行订亲——

那往往是赔本的买卖；

而且轮到他们自己做主，

反倒挑得更不划算。

这些都对；只是你办事的路数，

靠骗也好靠抢也好，

实在荒唐透顶：话一出口，

这计就注定办不成了，

正如设饵的诱子先把自己

要耍的花招嚷嚷出去，就再也骗不着人。

婚姻说到底不过是一纸誓言，

男人们不是背弃就是屈从：

那么连求婚都要赌假咒的人，

还有什么干不出来？

这种人事先又赌咒又说谎，

拿来做叛卖的信物，

而且宁可把罪加一等，

也不肯认一条罪？

就像窃贼，判决已定，

还坚称清白到底；

罪行已被证人当堂

指证得明明白白，

可这些可怜虫临到上绞架，

还要拿性命再撒一个谎。

你向神师告解的那几桩德行，

也不像你猜的那样

无足轻重，可以一边

无耻地抵赖、一边就算洗白；

仿佛你以为自己的话

在案子的两头都能正面命中；

又仿佛一位了不起的“驿站游侠骑士”

背信吃言、

赌咒说自己能隔着两寸厚的木板听见动静，

信誉也不会破产；

他能一面承认一面否认同一件事，

为战利品正着反着赌咒；

能让福音书替他跑腿，

帮他渡过伪誓的难关；

福音书才被按住亲吻，

就转手被出卖，像基督一样。

你们就是打着这些德行的旗号，

声言对全世界拥有权利，

大言不惭地要求

在恩宠和天性上管辖一切女性；

而且不把全天下的同性

尽数置于你们的暴虐之下，决不满足。

只是你会发现这省份难治得很，

凭你全部的奸谋诡计，

要统治这么庞大的一群——

而她们如今正一个一个地统治着你；

因为就算你们个个都是所罗门，

===Original===

And wise and great as he was once,

You’ll find they’re able to subdue

(As they did him) and baffle you.
And if you are impos’d upon,

’Tis by your own temptation done,

That with your ignorance invite,

And teach us how to use the slight;

For when we find y’ are still more taken

With false attracts of our own making,

Swear that’s a rose, and that a stone,

Like sots, to us that laid it on,

And what we did but slightly prime,

Most ignorantly daub in rhyme;

You force us, in our own defences,

To copy beams and influences;

To lay perfections on the graces,

And draw attracts upon our faces,

And, in compliance to your wit,

Your own false jewels counterfeit:

For by the practice of those arts

We gain a greater share of hearts;

And those deserve in reason most

That greatest pains and study cost:

For great perfections are, like heaven,

Too rich a present to be given.

Nor are these master-strokes of beauty

To be perform’d without hard duty,

Which, when they’re nobly done and well,

The simple natural excel.

How fair and sweet the planted rose

Beyond the wild in hedges grows!

For without art the noblest seeds

Of flow’rs degen’rate into weeds.

How dull and rugged, ere ’tis ground

And polish’d looks a diamond!

Though Paradise were e’er so fair,

It was not kept so without care.

The whole world, without art and dress,

Would be but one great wilderness;

And mankind but a savage herd,

For all that nature has conferr’d:

This does but rough-hew, and design;

Leaves art to polish and refine.

Though women first were made for men,

Yet men were made for them agen;

For when (outwitted by his wife)

Man first turn’d tenant but for life,

If women had not interven’d,

How soon had mankind had an end!

And that it is in being yet,

To us alone you are in debt.

And where’s your liberty of choice,

And our unnatural no voice?

Since all the privilege you boast,

And falsely usurp’d, or vainly lost,

Is now our right; to whose creation

You owe your happy restoration;

And if we had not weighty cause

To not appear, in making laws,

We could, in spite of all your tricks,

And shallow, formal politics,

Force you our managements t’ obey,

As we to yours (in show) give way.

Hence ’tis that, while you vainly strive

T’ advance your high prerogative,

You basely, after all your braves,

Submit, and own yourselves our slaves;

===Chinese===

又明智又伟大一如他当年，

你也会发觉她们照样能降服你

（她们当年就这样降服了他）、把你戏耍。

你若受了骗，

那也是你自招的，

是你的无知发出邀请、

教会我们这套手法；

因为眼见你们对

我们自造的假魅力越发入迷，

赌咒说那是玫瑰、那是宝石，

像醉鬼一样夸我们这些涂抹的人，

我们不过轻轻打的底色，

你们就用诗笨手笨脚地浓涂艳抹；

你们这是逼得我们为自卫起见，

描出光彩和神韵；

给三容四貌添上十全十美，

往脸蛋上勾出倾国倾城，

顺着你们的才智，

把你们的假珠宝翻成真货：

因为练熟了这套手艺，

我们赢得的心就更多；

道理很明白：花的心血和功夫最大，

就配得最多的报偿：

因为大的完美同天堂一样，

贵重得没法白送人。

何况这些美的大手笔

不下苦功做不成，

而一旦做得高贵漂亮，

天然本色就只能靠边。

园里栽的玫瑰开得

何等娇艳芬芳，篱边野生的怎比得上！

因为没有园艺，最高贵的花种

也会退化成莠草。

钻石未经琢磨打磨之前，

看着何等黯淡粗粝！

伊甸园纵然再美，

也是靠照料才保持住的。

整个世界若没有艺术和装点，

不过一片大荒野；

人类也不过一群野牲口，

任凭天生条件再好：

天性只管开出粗坯、画出草图；

把打磨提纯留给艺术。

女人固然最初是为男人造的，

可男人接着又是为女人造的；

因为当初（叫妻子骗了）

男人头一回只落个终身租户的名分，

若不是女人从中斡旋，

人类怕不早就断了种！

而人类至今还在，

这一笔账单算在我们头上。

你们自诩的选择自由在哪儿？

我们又何来“违背天性”的没有发言权？

你们夸口的全部特权——

或诈取、或昏失——如今是我们的权利；

你们的幸福复辟，

正是拜我们这一造所赐；

若不是我们有充分的理由

不肯在立法时露面，

任凭你们有多少花招

和浅薄做作的权术，我们也能

逼你们服从我们的治理，

就像我们（表面上）让步于你们。

所以，你们一面枉费心机地

抬高自己那套君权，

一面却在逞尽威风之后

卑鄙地屈服，自认是我们的奴隶；

===Original===

And ’cause we do not make it known,

Nor publicly our int’rest own,

Like sots, suppose we have no shares

In ord’ring you and your affairs;

When all your empire and command

You have from us at second hand;

As if a pilot, that appears

To sit still only while he steers,

And does not make a noise and stir,

Like ev’ry common mariner,

Knew nothing of the card, nor star,

And did not guide the man-of-war;

Nor we, because we don’t appear

In councils, do not govern there;

While, like the mighty Prester John,219

Whose person none dares look upon,

But is preserv’d in close disguise,

From being made cheap to vulgar eyes,

W’ enjoy as large a pow’r unseen,

To govern him, as he does men;

And in the right of our Pope Joan,

Make Emp’rors at our feet fall down:

Or Joan de Pucel’s braver name,220

Our right to arms and conduct claim;

Who, though a Spinster, yet was able

To serve France for a Grand Constable.
We make and execute all laws,

Can judge the judges and the cause;

Prescribe all rules of right or wrong

To th’ long robe, and the longer tongue,

’Gainst which the world has no defence;

But our more pow’rful eloquence.

We manage things of greatest weight

In all the world’s affairs of state;

Are ministers of war and peace,

That sway all nations how we please.

We rule all churches and their flocks,

Heretical and orthodox;

And are the heavenly vehicles

O’ th’ spirits in all conventicles.

By us is all commerce and trade

Improv’d, and manag’d, and decay’d;

For nothing can go off so well,

Nor bears that price, as what we sell.

We rule in ev’ry public meeting,

And make men do what we judge fitting;

Are magistrates in all great towns,

Where men do nothing but wear gowns.

We make the man-of-war strike sail,

And to our braver conduct veil,

And, when h’ has chas’d his enemies,

Submit to us upon his knees.

Is there an officer of state

Untimely rais’d, or magistrate,

That’s haughty and imperious?

He’s but a journeyman to us.

That as he gives us cause to do ’t,

Can keep him in, or turn him out.
We are your guardians, that increase

Or waste your fortunes how we please;

And, as you humour us, can deal

In all your matters, ill or well.
’Tis we that can dispose, alone,

Whether your heirs shall be your own,

To whose integrity you must,

In spite of all your caution, trust;

And, ’less you fly beyond the seas,

===Chinese===

只因我们不声张，

不公开认领我们的利益，

你们就像醉鬼一样认定

我们在你和你的事务里没有份；

殊不知你们的全部威权和号令，

都是从我们手里二手批来的；

这就好比一位舵手，

掌舵时只是安安稳稳地坐着，

不像每个普通水手那样

吵吵嚷嚷、上蹿下跳，

就以为他不懂海图和星象，

没在驾驶那艘战舰；

也不因为我们不出席

枢密会议，就说我们不主事；

我们正如那位威严的祭司王约翰，219

真容无人敢看，

始终蒙着严密的帷幕，

以免叫俗眼看得廉价，

我们隐身行使的权力，

治他如他治人，一样宽广；

我们凭我们的女教皇琼（Pope Joan）的权利，

叫皇帝们跪倒在脚下：

或者凭着圣女贞德（Joan de Pucel）更英武的名字，220

主张我们掌兵符、统全军的权利：

她虽是个纺纱老姑娘，

却照样为法兰西挂帅统兵。

法律的制定和执行全归我们，

能审判法官和案子；

向长袍和更长的舌头——

律师和辩士——颁布

一切是非的准则；

世人拿他们毫无办法，

只有我们更雄辩的口舌能降住。

世上一切最要紧的国务，

都由我们操持；

我们是战争与和平的执掌者，

万国兴废，全凭我们的意思。

我们统治一切教会和羊群，

异端的和正统的一概在列；

我们还是各处秘密聚会里

众灵体的天载神舆。

一切商业和贸易的

兴衰荣枯都由我们；

因为再没有比我们卖的东西

更抢手、更值钱的了。

一切公共集会都由我们主事，

男人们照我们的意思行事；

一切大城里我们都是执法官，

男人们只管穿着长袍坐堂。

我们叫战舰降帆，

向更高明的我们俯首；

等他追完了敌舰，

还得跪着向我们投降。

有没有哪位青云直上的

国家大臣或地方长官

倨傲专横、不可一世？

他不过是给我们打工的。

凭他给不给面子，

我们留他也行，轰他走也行。

我们是你们的监护人，

你们的家产由着我们增减盈缩；

看你们哄不哄我们高兴，

你们的一切事务我们办好也行办砸也行。

唯独我们能定夺

你们的继承人是不是你们亲生的，

而他们的忠厚，

你们纵有千般提防也只得信任；

除非你远走海外，

===Original===

Can fit you with what heirs we please;

And force you t’ own ’em, though begotten

By French valets, or Irish footmen.

Nor can the rigoroursest course

Prevail, unless to make us worse;

Who still, the harsher we are us’d,

Are further off from b’ing reduc’d,

And scorn t’ abate, for any ills,

The least punctilios of our wills.

Force does but whet our wits t’ apply

Arts, born with us for remedy;

Which all your politics, as yet,

Have ne’er been able to defeat:

For when y’ have try’d all sorts of ways,

What fools d’ we make of you in plays!

While all the favours we afford,

Are but to girt you with the sword,

To fight our battles in our steads,

And have your brains beat out o’ your heads;

Encounter, in despite of nature,

And fight at once with fire and water,

With pirates, rocks, and storms, and seas,

Our pride and vanity t’ appease;

Kill one another, and cut throats,

For our good graces, and best thoughts;

To do your exercise for honour,

And have your brains beat out the sooner;

Or crack’d, as learnedly, upon

Things that are never to be known;

And still appear the more industrious,

The more your projects are prepost’rous;

To square the circle of the arts,

And run stark mad to shew your parts;

Expound the oracle of laws,

And turn them which way we see cause;

Be our solicitors and agents,

And stand for us in all engagements.
And these are all the mighty pow’rs

You vainly boast to cry down ours,

And what in real value’s wanting,

Supply with vapouring and ranting;

Because yourselves are terrify’d,

And stoop to one another’s pride,

Believe we have as little wit

To be out-hector’d, and submit;

By your example, lose that right

In treaties which we gain’d in fight;

And, terrify’d into an awe,

Pass on ourselves a Salique law:221

Or, as some nations use, give place,

And truckle to your mighty race;

Let men usurp th’ unjust dominion,

As if they were the better women.

===Chinese===

不然我们想给你配什么继承人

就配什么继承人；

叫法国跟班也好、爱尔兰马夫

生的也罢，照样逼你认账。

任凭你们使出最严酷的手段

也不中用，只能叫我们变本加厉；

你们待我们越横，

我们离就范越远，

而且任凭什么灾祸，

也休想叫我们的意志

折损分毫体面。

强暴只会磨快我们的心智，

去使那生来就有的、专治男人的本领；

你们的全套权术，

至今没能破解这门功夫：

因为等你们十八般武艺都用尽，

看戏文里我们把你们耍成什么蠢样！

而我们赏给你们的全部恩典，

不过是替你们系上宝剑，

好替我们打仗，

打得你们脑浆迸裂；

违着天性去迎敌，

同时同水火两线作战；

同海盗、礁石、风暴和大海搏斗，

只求平息我们的骄矜和虚荣；

为了我们的青眼和好评，

你们互相残杀、白刃见红；

为了荣誉操练，

好叫脑浆早点迸出来；

或者煞有介事地把脑子

想破在永不可知的事情上；

而且你们的心思越是荒诞，

架势就显得越勤勉；

去化圆为方、贯通百艺，

为了显本事索性疯个透彻；

去解释法律的神谕，

朝着我们指定的方向扭；

做我们的诉状师和代理人，

一切债务场合都替我们出头。

这就是你们夸口的全部伟力，

枉费唇舌要压倒我们的伟力；

实际价值不够，

就拿大话和咆哮来凑；

只因你们自己吓破了胆，

一个个在彼此的骄傲面前弯腰，

就以为我们也一样没脑子，

会叫人吓住、乖乖屈服；

照你们的榜样，把我们

在战场上挣来的权利，

在谈判桌上输个精光；

吓成敬畏之后，

给自己立一部萨利克法：221

或者像某些民族那样让位，

向你们了不起的男种俯首帖耳；

任男人们僭取这不义的统治权，

仿佛他们才是更好的女人。

## To the Reader / 致读者
===Original===

*Poeta nascitur non fit*,1 is a sentence of as great truth as antiquity; it being most certain, that all the acquired learning imaginable is insufficient to complete a poet, without a natural genius and propensity to so noble and sublime an art. And we may, without offence, observe, that many very learned men, who have been ambitious to be thought poets, have only rendered themselves obnoxious to that satyrical inspiration our Author wittily invokes:

> Which made them, though it were in spite

Of nature and their stars, to write.

On the one side some who have had very little human learning, but were endued with a large share of natural wit and parts, have become the most celebrated (Shakespeare, D’Avenant, etc.) poets of the age they lived in. But, as these last are, *Rarae aves in terris*, so, when the muses have not disdained the assistances of other arts and sciences, we are then blessed with those lasting monuments of wit and learning, which may justly claim a kind of eternity upon earth. And our author, had his modesty permitted him, might, with Horace, have said,

> Exegi monumentum aere perennius:2

Or, with Ovid,

> Jamque opus exegi, quod nec Jovis ira, nec ignis,

Nec poterit ferrum, nec edax abolere vetustas.3

===Chinese===

*Poeta nascitur non fit*（诗人是天生的，不是造就的）1，这句话古旧而极真；因为可以断言：若无对这门高贵崇高艺术的天才与天性，任凭怎样后天的博学，都不足以成就一个诗人。我们也不妨不指名地指出：许多极有学问的人一心想被当作诗人，结果只是招来了我们作者机智祈请的那种讽刺的灵感：

> 那灵感叫他们违着天性
>
> 和星宿，硬要写诗。

另一方面，另一些人几乎未曾受过多少人文教育，却天赋着丰厚的天然才情，反而成了他们那个时代最负盛名的诗人（莎士比亚、达文南特等）。但正如后者是 *Rarae aves in terris*（世上的珍禽），所以当缪斯们不鄙薄其他艺术与科学的辅助时，我们便得享那些才学与智慧的不朽纪念碑——它们尽可在人间自称一种永恒。我们的作者，若非谦逊所限，本可像贺拉斯一样说：

> Exegi monumentum aere perennius（我建成了一座比青铜更永久的纪念碑）2

或者像奥维德一样：

> Jamque opus exegi, quod nec Jovis ira, nec ignis, nec poterit ferrum, nec edax abolere vetustas（如今我完成了这件作品，朱庇特的雷霆、烈火、铁刃和吞噬一切的岁月都不能将它销毁）3

===Original===

The Author of this celebrated Poem was of this his last composition: for although he had not the happiness of an academical education, as some affirm, if may be perceived, throughout his whole Poem, that he had read much, and was very well accomplished in the most useful parts of human learning.
Rapin (in his reflections) speaking of the necessary qualities belonging to a poet, tells us, he must have a genius extraordinary; great natural gifts; a wit just, fruitful, piercing, solid, and universal; an understanding clear and distinct; an imagination neat and pleasant; an elevation of soul, that depends not only on art or study, but is purely the gift of heaven, which must be sustained by a lively sense and vivacity; judgment to consider wisely of things, and vivacity for the beautiful expression of them, etc.
Now, how justly this character is due to our Author, we leave to the impartial reader, and those of nicer judgment, who had the happiness to be more intimately acquainted with him.
The reputation of this incomparable Poem is so thoroughly established in the world, that it would be superfluous, if not impertinent, to endeavour any panegyric upon it. King Charles II whom the judicious part of mankind will readily acknowledge to be a sovereign judge of wit, was so great an admirer of it, that he would often pleasantly quote it in his conversation. However, since most men have a curiosity to have some account of such anonymous authors, whose compositions have been eminent for wit or learning, we have, for their information, subjoined a short Life of the Author.

===Chinese===

这部名诗的作者正属于最后这一类：因为尽管如某些人所言，他未曾有幸受大学教育，但通读全诗便可察觉，他读书极多，在人文之学最有用的各门上都有极好的修养。

拉潘（在其《反思录》中）谈到诗人必备的素质时告诉我们：他必须有超凡的天才；深厚的天然禀赋；一种公正、丰饶、犀利、扎实而通达的才智；清晰明澈的理解力；纯净可喜的想象力；一种灵魂的昂扬——它不单靠艺术或研习，而纯是上天的恩赐，还须有活跃的感受力和生气来支撑；有对事物深思熟虑的判断力，又有把它们漂亮表达出来的生气，等等。

至于这份赞辞用在我们的作者身上何等公允，我们留给公正的读者，以及那些有幸与他过从较密、眼光更精的评判者。

这部无与伦比的诗的声誉早已在世上确立，再对它作任何颂扬，不说冒失，至少也是多余。查理二世国王——有识见的人们都乐于承认他是才智的最高裁判——就极爱此诗，常常在谈笑间信口援引。不过，既然多数人都好奇想知道这类匿名作者的来历——他们的作品以才智或学问著称——我们便为满足这份好奇心，附上了作者的一篇小传。
